**Holding and operative rule:** Section 1392(d)'s express restriction requires an in-effect federal motor-vehicle safety standard governing the same aspect of performance as the asserted state duty. The asserted antilock-equipment defect claims are not expressly displaced on this record because that federal predicate is absent; the Court does not decide whether common-law duties themselves are safety standards within that provision.

**Authority:** Justice Ginsburg's opinion of the Court, joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Breyer: eight direct rationale votes. Justice Scalia concurs only in the judgment and gives no separate explanation.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the express clause must be applied to the particular aspect of performance actually regulated. Easterwood requires attention to federal coverage of the duty at issue, and Cipollone requires examination of the enacted preemption language and predicate duty. Regulation of vehicle braking generally does not establish an operative antilock requirement for these claims. Paccar judicially invalidated inadequately supported stopping-distance requirements; that invalidation does not itself supply a federal safety standard forbidding state liability for omitted antilock equipment. Section 1392(d)'s condition is therefore unsatisfied without deciding whether common-law duties fall within its category of state standards. Section 1397(k) states that compliance with a federal safety standard does not exempt a person from common-law liability. The Court preserves that text but need not determine its every effect where an operative standard exists. The common-law claims return for ordinary adjudication; this statutory conclusion establishes neither a product defect nor causation and does not disturb the independent Georgia RICO judgment.

**Precedent treatment:**

- CSX Transportation, Inc. v. Easterwood, April 21, 1993: applies its attention to the aspect of performance actually covered, without importing the separate railroad speed holding or treating every braking rule as coverage of antilock omission.
- Cipollone v. Liggett Group, Inc., June 24, 1992: applies clause- and duty-specific analysis; its particular cigarette holdings do not answer all Safety Act questions.
- Paccar, Inc. v. National Highway Traffic Safety Administration, 573 F.2d 632: treats its judicial invalidation as the regulatory-history predicate, not as a new federal right to avoid common-law liability.

### Judicial invalidation alone establishes no actual federal conflict

**Holding and operative rule:** An express preemption clause does not categorically foreclose inquiry into an independently established actual conflict with operative federal law or a demonstrated federal objective. Here neither impossibility nor interference with such an objective is established by judicial invalidation and the absence of an operative antilock requirement, so the asserted conflict does not bar these common-law claims.

**Authority:** Justice Ginsburg's opinion of the Court, joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Breyer: eight direct rationale votes. Scalia's judgment-only agreement supplies no additional rationale vote.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the failure of express preemption does not end every possible conflict inquiry. The Eleventh Circuit read Cipollone too broadly when it treated Congress's express clause as invariably excluding implied preemption. Gade rejects automatic displacement from regulatory overlap but supplies no physical-impossibility-only rule. The manufacturers nevertheless must identify the federal obligation or policy that their asserted state duty would frustrate. Their strongest claim treats the ability to sell trucks without antilock equipment as a protected federal choice. Paccar does not establish that premise: the court rejected the agency's evidentiary support while allowing adequately supported future standards. It did not adopt a policy insulating manufacturers from state liability. Compliance with the asserted duty is not shown to violate federal law, and regulatory silence alone does not demonstrate the claimed obstacle. A valid operative standard or demonstrated affirmative policy requiring a uniform absence of regulation could present a different case. The Court leaves that case, and the ultimate tort merits here, undecided.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Cipollone v. Liggett Group, Inc., June 24, 1992: rejects the lower court's universal inference that any express provision exhausts every possible actual-conflict inquiry; its enacted-clause holdings remain intact.
- Gade v. National Solid Wastes Management Association, June 18, 1992: preserves the shared rule against automatic displacement from overlap; no physical-impossibility-only theory or broader separate theory is made Court law.
- Paccar, Inc. v. National Highway Traffic Safety Administration, 573 F.2d 632: distinguishes inadequate support for a mandatory standard from an affirmative policy protecting noninstallation against state duties.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Section 1397(k)'s compliance saving clause is not held to immunize every common-law action from an actual conflict. The Court does not decide whether tort duties are standards under §1392(d), every effect of the saving clause when a standard exists, or a federal field-wide ceiling. It does not find all air-brake regulation absent, invent an agency policy from an unproduced revision, or decide defect, accident causation, consortium entitlement, wrongful-death recovery, or damages.

#### Precedent Treatment

Easterwood and Cipollone require attention to actual coverage and the enacted clause. Cipollone is not read as universally foreclosing an independent actual-conflict inquiry. Gade supplies no automatic displacement from overlap and no Court-wide physical-impossibility-only rule. Paccar's judicial invalidation does not establish a protected federal freedom from the asserted tort duties.

#### Law After Decision

The presented claims survive the asserted express and conflict-preemption grounds because no applicable in-effect antilock standard or demonstrated conflicting federal command or objective establishes displacement. A saving clause preserves its actual terms without supplying immunity from every actual conflict. Valid operative standards and proved affirmative no-regulation policies remain distinct possibilities; no product-liability merits finding follows.

### Heintz v. Jenkins, No. 94-367. — 1995-04-18

Authority: [Record](../records/Heintz_v_Jenkins_merits_1995-04-18.md#public-projection).

#### Holdings

### Regular attorney debt collectors remain covered when they use litigation

**Holding and operative rule:** The FDCPA applies to an attorney who regularly collects or attempts to collect covered consumer debts owed or asserted to be owed another, including through litigation, subject to the statute's actual definition, exceptions, elements and defenses. There is no categorical attorney-litigation exemption; the decision does not cover lawyers merely because they practice law or establish that this settlement letter violated the Act.

**Authority:** Justice Souter's opinion of the Court, joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer: nine direct rationale and judgment votes.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that collection litigation is a means of collecting a debt within the enacted definition. The statute covers regular direct or indirect collection for another, and Congress deleted the former attorney exemption in 1986. Gardner supports enforcing those retained conditions without restoring an exclusion Congress removed. The strongest contrary argument is that rules regulating collection threats and communications can interact awkwardly with ordinary judicial proceedings. That concern does not justify excluding every litigating attorney from the definition. Congress retained an activity-specific exclusion for serving or attempting to serve legal process in connection with judicial debt enforcement, not a general occupational exclusion. Questions about a particular threat, communication or defense must be decided under the provision actually presented. An unsuccessful collection action is not automatically unlawful, and coverage does not prove the alleged insurance demand false or unauthorized. The complaint therefore returns for the ordinary inquiries into regularity, contractual authorization, alleged misrepresentation and defenses, without a liability or damages finding.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Brown v. Gardner, December 12, 1994: applies respect for enacted conditions and rejection of an added category restriction; it does not create a new FDCPA proof rule or resolve conduct liability.
- Green v. Hocking, 9 F.3d 18: rejects the lower-court categorical litigation-exemption approach as inconsistent with the enacted definition and attorney-exemption repeal; it supplies no continuing exemption in this Court's law.

**Operative statutory terms and questions not reached:** A covered debt is an obligation or alleged obligation of a consumer to pay money arising from a transaction whose money, property, insurance or services are primarily for personal, family or household purposes, whether or not reduced to judgment. The retained §1692a(6)(D) exception concerns a person while serving or attempting to serve legal process on another in connection with judicial enforcement of a debt; it does not create a whole-attorney exemption. No factual statutory exception is established here.

Section 1692e prohibits false, deceptive or misleading representations in debt collection; §1692f(1) addresses collection of an amount not expressly authorized by the agreement creating the debt or permitted by law. The alleged $4,173 charge remains to be tested under those provisions. Section 1692e(5) concerns threatened action that cannot legally be taken or is not intended; failure to prevail alone does not necessarily satisfy it. No authoritative resolution of those conduct applications is made.

The communication and defense provisions invoked in the objection retain their exact conditions. Under §1692c(c), a consumer's written refusal to pay or written request to cease communication bars further communications except to advise that collection efforts are terminated, to notify that the collector or creditor may invoke specified remedies ordinarily invoked by that collector or creditor, or, where applicable, to notify that the collector or creditor intends to invoke a specified remedy. A mailed notice is complete on receipt. These are alternative permissions, not mandatory notices. Under §1692k(c), the collector must prove by a preponderance that a violation was unintentional and resulted from bona fide error despite procedures reasonably adapted to avoid it; no general immunity for legal error is decided. Under §1692k(e), good-faith conformity with an FTC advisory opinion protects an act or omission even if the opinion is later amended, rescinded or held invalid; staff commentary is not automatically such an opinion. Section 814(d) denies the FTC and the other agencies assigned enforcement responsibility under §814(b) authority to promulgate trade-regulation rules or other regulations concerning collection of debts by debt collectors as defined in the Act. No defense is established on these allegations, and no comprehensive litigation exception or fee entitlement is adopted.

#### Precedent Treatment

Gardner supports applying the statutory conditions without a judicial category exemption. Green v. Hocking's contrary lower-court exemption is rejected. The decision settles regular-attorney/litigation coverage, not authoritative interpretations of every conduct-regulating provision or defense.

#### Law After Decision

Effective April 18, 1995, an attorney's regular collection of covered consumer debts for another is not exempt because it occurs through litigation. Coverage remains distinct from the ordinary elements and defenses of a particular claim. Neither a failed lawsuit nor the presence of a settlement letter automatically creates liability, privilege, good faith, damages or fees. The actual statutory exceptions operate on their stated terms, and no comprehensive litigation exemption is created.

### Lanphere & Urbaniak v. Colorado, No. 94-38. — 1995-04-18

Authority: [Record](../records/Lanphere_and_Urbaniak_v_Colorado_merits_1995-04-18.md#public-projection).

#### Holdings

### The intended-speech condition requires commercial-speech review

**Holding and operative rule:** When a State otherwise makes covered records available, a rule denying access and prohibiting use because the requester intends truthful, lawful commercial solicitation is subject to the governing commercial-speech standard. The absence of a general entitlement to every government record does not remove this speech-selected condition from First Amendment review; a qualified right to particular court records is left undecided.

**Authority:** Justice Kennedy's opinion of the Court, joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer: seven direct rationale votes. Justices O'Connor and Scalia dissent from the application and judgment.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the challenged line concerns expression as well as access. Colorado prohibits using the covered records and their information to solicit business for pecuniary gain and conditions inspection on a promise against direct solicitation. Identical record requests thus receive different treatment because of the protected communication the requester plans to make. Central Hudson governs truthful, nonmisleading commercial expression concerning lawful activity. Shapero establishes that targeted legal-service mail falls within that protection, while Discovery Network requires justification for a selected commercial category. Colorado's custody of the records does not erase these requirements. Its strongest argument distinguishes access to information from later communication of information already acquired, but the challenged enactment regulates both access and use within an existing disclosure regime. The Court need not decide whether experience and logic independently guarantee access to these particular court materials. Nor does this holding compel disclosure of confidential files, create records, or forbid neutral access conditions. It requires review of the speech condition actually imposed.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Central Hudson Gas & Electric Corp. v. Public Service Commission, 447 U.S. 557: applies commercial-speech protection to truthful solicitation about lawful services.
- Shapero v. Kentucky Bar Association, 486 U.S. 466: applies protection for truthful targeted mail while distinguishing the separate source-of-information question.
- Cincinnati v. Discovery Network, March 24, 1993: applies review of a speech-selected commercial category notwithstanding governmental control of the facility involved.
- Houchins v. KQED, Inc., 438 U.S. 1: does not establish an absolute governmental privilege to attach this speech restriction to otherwise available records; no general access holding is added.
- Nixon v. Warner Communications, Inc., 435 U.S. 589: does not establish an absolute governmental privilege to attach this speech restriction to otherwise available records; no general access holding is added.
- Press-Enterprise Co. v. Superior Court, 478 U.S. 1: preserves the separate experience-and-logic inquiry for qualified access; its application to these records is not decided.

### Colorado has not demonstrated material advancement in this application

**Holding and operative rule:** For a restriction on truthful, lawful commercial solicitation, the State must demonstrate real harms and material advancement of its substantial interests in the relevant setting. Colorado's generalized privacy, abuse-prevention, and public-confidence justifications do not establish that this categorical use-and-access restriction materially advances those interests as applied to these plaintiffs' proposed direct mail.

**Authority:** Justice Kennedy's opinion of the Court, joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer: seven direct rationale votes. Justices O'Connor and Scalia dissent from the application and judgment.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that substantial interests do not dispense with proof of the connection between those interests and the chosen burden. Edenfield requires real harms and material advancement in the particular professional setting; it does not invariably demand empirical studies. Privacy for persons merely accused of offenses, prevention of solicitation abuse, and confidence in justice are substantial concerns. But Colorado has not shown why denying these otherwise available names and addresses to these speakers materially protects those interests. The lower court inferred meaningful privacy simply from the plaintiffs' persistence in seeking access. That inference does not establish the effect of the selected restriction. The conceded availability of the information through the El Paso County News further bears on that explanation, without creating a rule that every privacy measure must cover every source. Shapero distinguishes targeted mail from coercive personal confrontation. No finding of unlawful or misleading proposed mail answers that distinction here. Supported controls on actual deception, harassment, or protected information remain available.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Edenfield v. Fane, April 26, 1993: applies the real-harm and material-advancement burden without requiring studies in every case.
- Shapero v. Kentucky Bar Association, 486 U.S. 466: applies the distinction between targeted mail and coercive personal solicitation; the Court does not adjudicate unexamined letter-specific content.
- Cincinnati v. Discovery Network, March 24, 1993: preserves the need for a connection between the commercial selection and the harm, without an all-sources-at-once rule.
- Ibanez v. Florida Department of Business and Professional Regulation, June 13, 1994: applies the requirement of justification responsive to truthful professional expression; actual deception remains regulable.

### The categorical prohibition lacks the required reasonable fit

**Holding and operative rule:** The restriction must reasonably fit the State's substantial interests and may not burden more truthful, lawful commercial speech than that fit permits; the State need not employ the least restrictive means. This categorical prohibition and access assurance lack the required fit as applied, because Colorado has not justified selecting every proposed direct solicitation over neutral access protections or measures addressing actual abuses.

**Authority:** Justice Kennedy's opinion of the Court, joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer: seven direct rationale votes. Justices O'Connor and Scalia dissent from the application and judgment.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the wholesale speech criterion is broader than Colorado has justified. Fox requires a reasonable, proportionate fit rather than the least restrictive conceivable measure. Discovery Network requires the chosen category to correspond to the asserted harm. Colorado distinguishes the point of official disclosure from the later mailing and argues that withholding information imposes only an indirect burden. That distinction deserves consideration, but it cannot establish fit by itself when the statute also prohibits the information's solicitation use and selects otherwise identical requests by intended expression. The record does not explain why neutral redaction, delayed release, opt-out protections, retrieval fees, or direct regulation of deceptive, coercive, and harassing solicitation are inadequate to support a more fitted response. The Court does not mandate any particular replacement system or find that every alternative is required in every case. It rejects this categorical criterion on this record. Confidential records and independently justified neutral limits remain subject to their own rules.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Board of Trustees of State University of New York v. Fox, 492 U.S. 469: applies reasonable fit and preserves rejection of a least-restrictive-means requirement.
- Cincinnati v. Discovery Network, March 24, 1993: applies the relationship between the selected commercial category and the harm; alternatives are relevant without requiring regulation of all sources simultaneously.
- Shapero v. Kentucky Bar Association, 486 U.S. 466: preserves appropriately directed safeguards against misleading or abusive mail instead of an unsupported blanket barrier.
- Edenfield v. Fane, April 26, 1993: retains protection for materially different professional settings and supported regulation of deception or coercion.

#### Precedent Treatment

Central Hudson and Fox remain the governing framework. Discovery Network, Edenfield, and Ibanez supply the selected-category, material-advancement, and truthful-professional-speech principles applied here. Shapero protects targeted mail without alone determining access to government information. Houchins and Nixon do not turn the absence of a general access entitlement into an absolute privilege to burden speech; the Court leaves the separate qualified-access inquiry under Press-Enterprise undecided. No precedent is overruled, and no strict-scrutiny or commercial/political-speech equality rule is adopted.

#### Law After Decision

Effective April 18, 1995, the challenged solicitation-use prohibition and access assurance cannot sustain withholding otherwise available information in this application under Central Hudson and Fox. Material advancement and reasonable fit remain distinct requirements; each deficient showing supports the judgment. The ruling does not require disclosure of confidential records, resolve access to every court filing or proceeding, command creation of a list, or invalidate independently justified neutral access rules. Regulation of false, deceptive, coercive, or harassing solicitation remains available. Seven Justices directly join the controlling explanation; no Marks inquiry is needed.

### Plaut v. Spendthrift Farm, Inc., No. 93-1121. — 1995-04-18

Authority: [Record](../records/Plaut_v_Spendthrift_Farm_Inc_merits_1995-04-18.md#public-projection).

#### Holdings

### Section 27A(b) cannot reopen this completed private federal judgment

**Holding and operative rule:** Article III prevents §27A(b) from requiring reinstatement of this private §10(b) action after its dismissal with prejudice became final without appeal before December 19, 1991. Satisfaction of the statute's reinstatement conditions does not overcome that constitutional limit; the denial of reinstatement is affirmed without deciding pending cases, judgments still on direct review, continuing prospective relief, or independently lawful judicial reopening.

**Authority:** Justice Thomas's opinion of the Court, joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, and Breyer: seven direct rationale and judgment votes. Stone-Zsela also concurs separately; his broader formulation is not the Court's holding. Justices Stevens and Ginsburg dissent.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that Morgan Stanley governs this completed private limitations judgment. That decision accepts Congress's genuine amendment of limitations law but rejects this command to reopen a dismissal already final without appeal. Robertson permits changed law in pending litigation; it does not erase the completed-judgment boundary. McCullough recognizes completed adjudication, and Klein protects the judicial function without prohibiting genuine statutory amendment. The plaintiffs satisfied §27A(b)'s conditions and timely sought reinstatement, so their motion fails on Article III grounds, not statutory ineligibility. Their strongest argument is that Congress restored earlier filing expectations while leaving fraud and defenses for judicial resolution. Morgan Stanley considered that distinction: further merits work cannot make this completed dismissal nonfinal. No altered statutory command, relevant record difference, intervening displacement, or demonstrated unworkability warrants reconsideration here. Wheeling Bridge concerns continuing prospective relief, and Sioux Nation concerns the Government's waiver of its own defense. The Court applies the existing confined rule, leaving broader remedial legislation, other statutory applications, due process, and fraud liability undecided.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Morgan Stanley & Co. v. Pacific Mutual Life Insurance Co., May 23, 1994: applies its as-applied Article III rule to the same completed, unappealed private-dismissal setting without enlarging it.
- Robertson v. Seattle Audubon Society, March 25, 1992: preserves genuine legislative changes governing pending cases; it does not authorize the present reopening of a completed judgment.
- McCullough v. Virginia, 172 U.S. 102: applies the distinction between changing law and disturbing completed adjudication.
- United States v. Klein, 80 U.S. (13 Wall.) 128: preserves protection of the judicial function without treating actual legislative amendment as forbidden.
- Pennsylvania v. Wheeling & Belmont Bridge Co., 59 U.S. (18 How.) 421: distinguishes adjustment of the continuing prospective operation of equitable relief.
- United States v. Sioux Nation of Indians, 448 U.S. 371: distinguishes sovereign waiver of the Government's own final-judgment defense from compulsory reopening between private parties.
- Chase Securities Corp. v. Donaldson, 325 U.S. 304: leaves its due-process revival rule intact; it does not resolve the Article III question presented.

**Statutory conditions and reservations:** Section 27A(b) addresses a private §10(b) action commenced on or before June 19, 1991, dismissed as time barred after that date, and timely under the law of the jurisdiction, including retroactivity principles, as it existed June 19, 1991. It directs reinstatement on a plaintiff's motion within sixty days after December 19, 1991. These conditions were satisfied here, including the February 11, 1992 motion. Subsection (a), other applications of subsection (b), general severability, constitutional due process, fraud, damages, federal waiver, and independently authorized judicial reopening are not decided. The Court adopts no comprehensive prohibition governing every later legislative effect on every final order.

#### Precedent Treatment

Morgan Stanley controls without enlargement. Robertson retains legislative amendment of pending-case law; McCullough and Klein protect completed adjudication without denying that genuine amendment occurred. Wheeling Bridge and Sioux Nation remain distinct prospective-relief and sovereign-waiver settings. Chase Securities remains a due-process revival authority. No precedent is overruled.

#### Law After Decision

The existing May 23, 1994 Article III rule remains operative: §27A(b) cannot compel reopening of the completed, unappealed private dismissal presented here. This decision applies that rule rather than establishing a universal prohibition on every legislative effect on every final order. Stone-Zsela's broader formulation and the two dissenters' proposed reconsideration are noncontrolling. Statutory timeliness is satisfied, and neither fraud nor liability is adjudicated.

### Shalala v. Whitecotton, No. 94-372. — 1995-04-18

Authority: [Record](../records/Shalala_v_Whitecotton_merits_1995-04-18.md#public-projection).

#### Holdings

### The Table onset route requires the first manifestation of the injury

**Holding and operative rule:** A claimant using the applicable Table's onset route must establish that the first symptom or manifestation of onset of the claimed Table injury occurred within the prescribed period after vaccination; for the DPT encephalopathy claim here, that period is three days. A later manifestation of the same already manifested injury does not satisfy first onset, but no finding that Maggie had that injury before vaccination is made or insulated from review by this construction.

**Authority:** Justice O'Connor's opinion of the Court, joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer: nine direct rationale votes.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that first onset means the beginning of the claimed injury, not simply its first manifestation after vaccination. The statutory timing requirement would lose its work if any later symptom of the same injury sufficed. Gardner requires respect for an enacted condition while preserving the protection that follows its satisfaction. The Table relieves a qualifying claimant of proving the vaccine's actual causal mechanism; it does not eliminate the qualifying onset requirement. The lower court's comparison with the residual-seizure provision does not overcome the first-onset language. Nor does the separate unrelated-factor defense require the contrary reading: a prior cause need not have produced an earlier manifestation of the Table injury. Significant aggravation remains a separate route for an injury already present. The Court therefore reverses the compensation direction without deciding whether the master's preexisting-injury finding is factually sound. A small head circumference or other prior abnormality alone does not establish the identity, onset, cause, or aggravation of this child's injury.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Brown v. Gardner, December 12, 1994: applies respect for distinct statutory conditions without inserting a universal actual-causation requirement into the Table route; its veterans-benefits rule is not transplanted.
- Daubert v. Merrell Dow Pharmaceuticals, Inc., June 28, 1993: leaves expert-evidence admissibility separate from the Vaccine Act's entitlement predicates; no new admissibility determination is made.

### Preserve significant aggravation and the separate rebuttal stage on remand

**Holding and operative rule:** A qualifying significant-aggravation claim and an otherwise available actual-causation claim remain distinct from first onset. The claimant's statutory entitlement showing by a preponderance precedes the Secretary's separate burden to establish an unrelated factor by a preponderance; the disputed preexisting-injury finding, preserved alternative routes, and consolidated new-evidence proceeding return for lawful consideration without an award, final denial, or hypothetical rebuttal ruling.

**Authority:** Justice O'Connor's opinion of the Court, joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer: nine direct rationale votes on the sequence and bounded remand.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that correcting the onset construction does not decide the family's remaining entitlement. Congress separately provided significant aggravation and actual causation, and the lower courts must consider properly preserved routes under their own requirements. Greenwich Collieries distinguishes persuasion, presumptions, and separately allocated defenses; the Vaccine Act itself supplies the governing allocations here, not the APA. The Secretary's unrelated-factor burden arises only after the necessary claimant showing. Section 13's exclusion of idiopathic, unexplained, unknown, hypothetical, and undocumentable causes from that defense does not dispense with the initial predicate. Conversely, a prior abnormality cannot automatically defeat compensation or shift the Secretary's burden to the claimant. The Court does not decide whether a specifically identified underlying condition may qualify as unrelated although its ultimate origin is unknown. That dispute and the sufficiency of any rebuttal are premature without a qualifying claim. The new-evidence procedural component likewise returns without an invented mootness or Rule 60 determination. The lawful remand preserves factual review and statutory alternatives, not a predetermined medical outcome.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Director, OWCP v. Greenwich Collieries, June 20, 1994: applies only the distinction among persuasion, presumptions, and separately allocated defenses; the Vaccine Act's own burdens govern.
- Brown v. Gardner, December 12, 1994: preserves the distinction between an eligibility condition and additional grounds not supplied by the statute; no compensation follows automatically from removing a legal error.

**Operative qualifications and nonreach:** Section 13(a)(2)(A) excludes any idiopathic, unexplained, unknown, hypothetical, or undocumentable cause, factor, injury, illness, or condition from unrelated factors. That provision is distinct from §14(b)(3)(B), which excludes an encephalopathy from the Table when a preponderance establishes that its cause is infection, toxins, trauma, or metabolic disturbances; those specified Table causes were not asserted by the Secretary in this appeal. No application of either provision is decided. The August 2, 1990 petition uses §2114(a) and (b) as in effect February 8, 1995: the March 10, 1995 revision applies only to petitions filed on or after March 10. The earlier Table and its qualifications both remain applicable. No new definition of significant aggravation, final preexisting-injury finding, actual-causation determination, or new-evidence merits ruling is adopted.

#### Precedent Treatment

Gardner supports keeping distinct statutory predicates and consequences separate. Greenwich Collieries supplies a distinction among burdens, not an imported APA allocation. Daubert remains separate evidentiary law and decides no medical or entitlement issue here. The Court does not resolve the lower Koston-related rebuttal dispute before it becomes necessary.

#### Law After Decision

First onset remains a true temporal predicate under the applicable Table, distinct from significant aggravation and actual causation. A qualifying claimant retains the statutory Table benefit without proving a vaccine mechanism; the Secretary retains the assigned preponderance rebuttal burden subject to the actual unrelated-factor exclusions. This decision corrects the onset construction without deciding medical entitlement or every rebuttal issue. The 1990 petition remains under the Table and qualifications effective February 8, 1995.

## Procedure after completed events

### United States v. Shabani; No. 93-981; 513 U.S. 10 — 1994-11-01

The case returns to the Ninth Circuit for its reserved sufficiency and other preserved appellate issues under the construction announced here. The overt-act objection supplies no ground for relief; no terminal judgment on the other claims is entered.

### U.S. Bancorp Mortgage Co. v. Bonner Mall Partnership; No. 93-714; 513 U.S. 18 — 1994-11-08

This Supreme Court proceeding is dismissed as moot. The Ninth Circuit judgment remains undisturbed. The Court enters no bankruptcy merits judgment, settlement-enforcement order or further remand.

### Hess v. Port Authority Trans-Hudson Corp., including Walsh; No. 93-1197; 513 U.S. 30 — 1994-11-14

Both federal employee actions may proceed against PATH, subject to ordinary defenses and adjudication of the underlying claims. The Eleventh Amendment premise of dismissal is removed. The Court awards no damages and decides no negligence, Boiler Inspection Act merits or independent nonimmunity limitations question.

### United States v. X-Citement Video, Inc.; No. 93-723; 513 U.S. 64 — 1994-11-29

The Ninth Circuit must apply the statutory construction, account for the existing minority-knowledge finding, and address the reserved indictment-scienter and actual-tape claims and properly presented implementation issues within its lawful review. The two renewed facial-definition grounds are rejected on the limited grounds stated. No acquittal, count-specific final affirmance, harmlessness conclusion or mandatory retrial is ordered.

### Church of Scientology Flag Service Organization, Inc. v. City of Clearwater, No. 93-1062. — 1994-12-05

The Eleventh Circuit judgment is vacated only as to its rejection of prospective relief against the identifying disclosures designated as section 100.03(1)(a), (b), (c), and (j), and section 100.05(1)(g). The matter returns to that court for RFRA adjudication and any district-court findings required. Existing Church-favorable relief and the purpose/severability remand remain undisturbed. Ordinary review here ends; proceedings continue below. No damages, refund payment, whole-ordinance injunction, new calendar deadline, or restored enforcement of invalidated provisions is ordered.

### Federal Election Commission v. NRA Political Victory Fund, No. 93-1151. — 1994-12-06

The existing writ is dismissed for want of jurisdiction. The lower judgment ending this enforcement action remains undisturbed; the district court's penalty and injunction are not reinstated. There is no Supreme Court merits remand or new adjudication of the unresolved enforcement defenses. This review proceeding is terminal; no additional Court act or filing condition is ordered.

### Reich v. Collins, No. 93-908. — 1994-12-06

The Georgia Supreme Court's denial of constitutionally adequate backward-looking relief is reversed, and the cause is remanded for a clear and certain lawful remedial opportunity. Ordinary proof of payment and amount remains. No sum, interest entitlement, deadline, or particular retroactive assessment is ordered. The accepted substantive tax defect is not retried by this mandate; relief proceeds below.

### Brown v. Gardner, No. 93-1128. — 1994-12-12

The Federal Circuit judgment is affirmed. The existing remand for administrative redetermination of Gardner's eligibility continues without the invalid fault-or-accident prerequisite. No automatic compensation, medical causal finding, refund, deadline, or broader regulatory rewrite is ordered. Ordinary Supreme Court review ends; the administrative claim remains for decision under the lawful conditions.

### In re Baby K; No. 93-1673. — 1994-12-12

The Fourth Circuit judgment and prospective declaration remain in force at the stated emergency-treatment scope. The hospital must provide necessary respiratory stabilization within its capabilities unless an applicable statutory compliance route is satisfied; a compliant transfer is permitted, not compelled. No damages, particular future transfer or broader care order is entered. ADA and Rehabilitation Act grounds remain unreviewed; CAPTA, general state-law duties and parental or guardian authority are not adjudicated.

### Nebraska Department of Revenue v. Loewenstein; No. 93-823. — 1994-12-12

The Nebraska Supreme Court's judgment concerning Revenue Ruling 22-85-1 is reversed and remanded. The state court shall uphold the challenged tax as applied to the identified repo earnings and conduct any remaining proceedings consistent with this decision. The unappealed ruling 22-87-4 remains outside the mandate. No tax amount, refund, add-back assessment or recomputation is ordered.

### Plakas v. Drinski; No. 93-1824. — 1995-01-09

Summary judgment for Drinski and Newton County remains in force. Supreme Court review ends without a trial order, damages award, further factfinding or separate remedial direction. A distinct provocation claim, unpleaded beating theory, independent municipal injury and qualified-immunity disposition are not adjudicated.

### Interstate Commerce Commission v. Transcon Lines; No. 93-1318; 513 U.S. 138. — 1995-01-10

The Ninth Circuit's judgment denying this credit-rule collection injunction is reversed. On remand, the lower courts must implement an injunction against collection of the admitted-unlawful loss-of-discount liquidated-damages class, specifying its operative terms and resolving genuine scope, inclusion or amount disputes beyond the concessions. The conceded original-notice failure, years-late revised billing, prohibited aggregation and liquidated-damages character are not reopened. Ordinary unpaid freight principal, other estate receivables and collections outside that class are excluded. A properly presented distinct lawful collection basis remains for its own adjudication; none is established by this judgment. The prior separate coded-rate component remains undisturbed. Supreme Court merits review is complete; implementation proceeds below, without a new deadline, exact dollar award, estate-distribution order or general discharge.

### Tome v. United States, No. 93-6892 — 1995-01-10

The mandate reverses the Tenth Circuit judgment and returns the case to that court to determine whether another preserved ground admits the statements and, if not, to apply ordinary harmless-error rules. Review here is complete; appellate proceedings continue. A new trial follows only if error requiring that relief is established.

### Young v. Northern Illinois Conference of United Methodist Church, No. 93-1917 — 1995-01-17

The Seventh Circuit's jurisdictional judgment is vacated and the case remanded for entry of a substantive dismissal of the stated ministerial advancement-and-retention claims. No new discrimination trial, ordination, reinstatement, promotion, reconsideration, money, or costs is ordered. The Chief would additionally affirm dismissal on the merits and expressly direct dismissal with prejudice. Review here is complete; entry of the corrected judgment remains below.

### Allied-Bruce Terminix Cos. v. Dobson; No. 93-1001. — 1995-01-18

The Alabama Supreme Court judgment is reversed and the cause remanded. The state courts must apply the actual-commerce rule and address formation, consent, fraud, duress, unconscionability, severability and other ordinary contract issues insofar as properly presented and preserved, as well as clause scope and implementing procedure. No waived defense is revived, final damages ordered or universal party-by-party arbitration order entered. The cause returns to the Illinois courts after partial affirmance and partial reversal of the interlocutory judgment. The prior rejection of an injunction remains undisturbed. Supreme Court merits review ends; the Court makes no final liability, damages or precise blackout-date finding.

The Federal Circuit judgment is reversed and the case returns for proceedings consistent with the Court's construction and seven-Justice determination that these sales fail the saved-seed condition. The Ninth Circuit judgment and the retrial direction based on categorical nonwaivability are reversed. The case returns for ordinary lawful implementation of the bounded impeachment decision. The Sixth Circuit must return the case for proceedings consistent with the liability/remedy distinction. ### Schlup v. Delo; No. 93-7901. — 1995-01-23

The Eighth Circuit judgment is vacated. That court must remand to the District Court for the proper gateway assessment, including a reasoned decision about factual development. The existing videotape and new timing and identification accounts must be considered together; the Court predetermines no credibility finding, gateway success or hearing requirement. Otherwise barred constitutional claims may proceed only within the gateway and other governing conditions, and passing the gateway proves none of their merits. Supreme Court review ends; the habeas proceeding continues below without an automatic writ, retrial, release or stay.

### Fargo Women’s Health Organization v. Schafer, No. 93-1712. — 1995-02-13

The Eighth Circuit must preserve the affirmed rejection of the specified definition-vagueness and limited penalty grounds and return the access claims to the District Court. The District Court must apply the complete Casey standard to each challenged access duty on a properly supported record, identify its actual burdened class, assess attributable additional interference against real conditions, and consider interacting requirements together without double counting. It must promptly consider the providers' interim-relief request under the ordinary standard and current record, given the pre-enforcement penal posture. No existing injunction is automatically reinstated, and no hearing, trial, final severance, permanent injunction or damages award is ordered. Supreme Court review ends; the access proceeding and lawful remedial work continue below. The affirmed grounds do not foreclose a materially different authoritative construction or concrete enforcement challenge.

### Lebron v. National Railroad Passenger Corp., No. 93-1525. — 1995-02-21

The mandate reverses the Second Circuit judgment and remands for proceedings consistent with Amtrak's governmental status for First Amendment purposes. The lower courts must decide the unresolved constitutional and remedial issues and reconsider the associated state-law dispositions under ordinary law. The Court does not reinstate the mandatory-display injunction, set a display deadline, award damages, determine a rent amount, or resolve either side's final contract rights. Review here ends; proceedings continue below.

Supreme Court review of the admitted question is complete. The Sixth Circuit judgment is vacated on the challenged combined-error disposition. Harmlessness must be assessed on the whole existing trial record with the State bearing the burden beyond a reasonable doubt. If a properly available constitutional violation is established and that showing fails, the writ shall issue for the affected conviction or convictions unless Ohio retries O'Neal within a reasonable time. The Court orders no immediate release, acquittal, unconditional writ, fixed retrial period or automatic restoration of the District Court's original order. Independent grounds outside the admitted question are not newly adjudicated.

The Alabama Supreme Court's judgment stands against the admitted challenge. No sentencing remand, new weighing proceeding, life-sentence order or guilt retrial is directed. ## Calendar and material dependencies

The inventory contains 99 matters (97 merits and two original proceedings), including fifteen established additional merits matters with assigned dockets. Hays is excluded. There are 106 distinct matters after the seven additional unscheduled carryovers are included. No petition-stage event or application appears among the 99 supplied scheduled events; carried applications remain open at their stated stages.

Dates below are the supplied inventory's planning dates, confirmed against Section I of each brief. They are not newly adjudicated Court actions or a certification of historical grants. The fifteen `+` matters use the supplied simulation-assigned dockets; their established merits posture is carried from Standing State without inventing grant dates or petition polls. Consolidated companions count once. No application or petition-stage event is listed among the 99 supplied events.

**Same-day grouping:** every equal event date is one same-day group, including groups split across chunks: 1995-01-10 (1/2), 1995-02-21 (2/3), 1995-04-19 (4/5), 1995-05-15 (5/6), 1995-06-05 (6/7), 1995-06-14 (7/8), and 1995-06-29 (8/9). Inventory order is a display tie-break only. No OT1994 coordinated release sequence is established at opening. Uncoordinated events on the same date use the common pre-group law, even when their Run tasks occur separately.

The April 19 matters share one entering-law baseline; no peer supplies earlier law without an established coordinated release sequence.
